{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01Q2Px7GfbKRHr7KzXsPR5Jg","type":"tool_result","content":"     1→# OpenEMR Foundation — Product Research\n     2→\n     3→Researched: 2026-02-16\n     4→Developer website: https://www.open-emr.org/\n     5→\n     6→## Overview\n     7→\n     8→OpenEMR is the most popular open-source electronic health records (EHR) and medical practice management platform in the world. Originally released in 2001 as \"MedicalPractice Professional (MP Pro)\" by Synitech, it was reworked for HIPAA compliance and released as OpenEMR under the GNU GPL in August 2002. The project was maintained by various community developers over the years; the nonprofit OEMR organization was formed in 2010, followed by the OpenEMR Foundation in April 2019 to serve as the current steward of the project.\n     9→\n    10→OpenEMR is used globally at an estimated 20,000+ healthcare facilities across 184 countries, serving over 90 million patients. In the US alone, there are an estimated 5,000+ installations serving 30+ million patients. Notable adopters include the U.S. Peace Corps (deployed across 77 countries via a five-year contract with EnSoftek) and the International Planned Parenthood Federation (IPPF). The software is primarily used by small to mid-size ambulatory practices, community health centers, and clinics, though it also serves hospitals in developing countries. It supports 34+ languages and runs on Windows, Linux, and macOS.\n    11→\n    12→The product is free and open-source, with no licensing fees. Revenue in the ecosystem comes from professional support vendors, hosted cloud services, and optional paid add-on modules (telehealth, fax/SMS, claims clearinghouse). The OpenEMR Foundation is a nonprofit and the project is community-driven, with contributions from volunteers and commercial supporters. The software is built on PHP, Apache, and MySQL/MariaDB.\n    13→\n    14→## Product: OpenEMR\n    15→\n    16→CHPL IDs: 10938 (version 7.0, certified 2022-07-08), 11757 (version 8, certified 2026-01-30)\n    17→\n    18→### What It Is\n    19→\n    20→OpenEMR is a full-featured ambulatory EHR and practice management system. It is ONC-certified as a Complete Ambulatory EHR. The certified module *is* the whole product — there is no separate commercial product wrapping it. Both CHPL listings represent successive versions of the same platform (7.0 and 8), with identical certified criteria covering clinical documentation (a)(1-5, 12, 14), transitions of care (b)(1), CQMs (c)(1-3), security/privacy (d)(1-13), patient access (e)(3), FHIR APIs (g)(2-10), and direct messaging (h)(1).\n    21→\n    22→The SED intended user description is \"Ambulatory clinic providers and staff.\" One required paid third-party service exists for ONC compliance: the EMR Direct phimail service (~$300 setup, ~$150/year per provider) for Direct secure messaging.\n    23→\n    24→### Users & Market\n    25→\n    26→**Primary users**: Physicians, clinical staff, billing staff, and practice managers at ambulatory clinics. Patients access the system via the built-in patient portal.\n    27→\n    28→**Clinical settings**: Predominantly small practices (1-10 staff), solo practitioners, community health centers, FQHCs, and small clinics. Also used in hospitals internationally, especially in developing countries. Some specialty use including ophthalmology/optometry (has dedicated eye care module), behavioral health (group therapy support), orthopedic, and dental practices (per user reviews).\n    29→\n    30→**Market position**: OpenEMR occupies a unique niche as the leading open-source ambulatory EHR. It competes not on features parity with enterprise EHRs (Epic, Cerner) but on cost (free), flexibility (fully customizable source code), and data ownership (self-hosted option). On Capterra, it holds a 3.9/5 rating (21 reviews) with 4.4/5 for value for money. On SoftwareFinder, 4.2/5 (31 reviews) with 58% giving 5 stars.\n    31→\n    32→**User feedback themes** (from Capterra and SoftwareFinder reviews):\n    33→- Pros: Free/low cost, full control over data, extensive customization, fast performance, reliable uptime, integrated billing makes it a complete practice management package\n    34→- Cons: Requires IT expertise to install and configure, steep learning curve, billing module needs enhancement for specialized practices, community support can be slow for urgent issues, complex codebase\n    35→\n    36→### Modules & Functionality\n    37→\n    38→Based on the vendor's feature pages, wiki documentation, and third-party descriptions, OpenEMR includes the following integrated modules:\n    39→\n    40→**Clinical / EHR:**\n    41→- Patient demographics and registration\n    42→- Encounter management with SOAP notes\n    43→- Medical issues/problems list\n    44→- Medications tracking\n    45→- Allergies\n    46→- Immunizations records\n    47→- Vitals with growth charts\n    48→- Review of systems\n    49→- Lab orders, results, and integration (automated lab interface)\n    50→- Procedures tracking\n    51→- Referrals management\n    52→- Clinical decision rules / alerts\n    53→- DICOM medical image viewer\n    54→- Eye/Ophthalmology/Optometry specialty module (dedicated form tables: `form_eye_*`)\n    55→- Group therapy support\n    56→- Graphical charting\n    57→- Electronic syndrome surveillance\n    58→\n    59→**Scheduling:**\n    60→- Appointment calendar with categories, colors, and type restrictions\n    61→- Repeating appointments\n    62→- Patient flow board, tracking, and reporting\n    63→- Find open appointment slots\n    64→- Patient appointment notifications (email/SMS)\n    65→- Recall/reminders board\n    66→- Multi-facility support\n    67→\n    68→**Prescriptions & e-Prescribing:**\n    69→- Prescription creation and tracking\n    70→- Online drug search (RXNORM integration)\n    71→- e-Prescribing to pharmacies\n    72→- Print, fax, and email distribution\n    73→- Customizable prescription layouts (DEA, NPI, state license numbers)\n    74→- In-house pharmacy dispensary support\n    75→\n    76→**Billing & Practice Management:**\n    77→- Flexible coding: CPT, HCPCS, ICD-9, ICD-10, SNOMED\n    78→- 5010 standards support\n    79→- Professional (CMS-1500) and institutional (UB-04) billing\n    80→- Electronic claim submission via clearinghouse integration\n    81→- Paper claims support\n    82→- Insurance eligibility queries\n    83→- Insurance tracking (multiple plans per patient)\n    84→- Accounts receivable interface\n    85→- EOB entry interface\n    86→- Automated 835/ERA (electronic remittance) posting\n    87→- Sales, collections, and insurance distribution reports\n    88→\n    89→**Patient Portal (built-in):**\n    90→- Patient self-registration\n    91→- View demographics, conditions, medications, allergies, lab results\n    92→- Appointment scheduling and cancellation (with staff confirmation)\n    93→- Secure email-style messaging with clinic staff\n    94→- Secure real-time chat with clinic staff\n    95→- Online payments and account ledger viewing\n    96→- Document center: fill out forms (privacy agreements, consents, medical history)\n    97→- Submit documents for staff review\n    98→- Download CCR/CCD continuity of care records\n    99→- Download lab documents\n   100→- Electronic signature capability\n   101→- CCDA support\n   102→\n   103→**Interoperability & Data Exchange:**\n   104→- CCDA import/export\n   105→- FHIR R4 API (US Core IG)\n   106→- SMART on FHIR support\n   107→- Direct secure messaging (via EMR Direct phimail)\n   108→- USCDI v5 support (as of late 2025 SVAP update)\n   109→\n   110→**Forms & Documentation:**\n   111→- Layout-based forms (LBF) system for custom form creation\n   112→- CAMOS (Computer Aided Medical Ordering System)\n   113→- Nation Notes (WYSIWYG editor)\n   114→- Template-driven forms\n   115→- Electronic digital document management\n   116→- Voice recognition ready (Windows)\n   117→\n   118→**Reporting:**\n   119→- Appointment, encounter, patient list reports\n   120→- Prescription and drug dispensing reports\n   121→- Referral and immunization reports\n   122→- Clinical Quality Measure (CQM) calculations (CMS22, CMS69, CMS122, CMS124, CMS125, CMS127, CMS130, CMS138, CMS147, CMS165)\n   123→- Automated Measure Calculations (AMC) and tracking\n   124→- Syndromic surveillance reports\n   125→- Procedure order statistics\n   126→- Insurance eligibility reports\n   127→\n   128→**Internal Messaging:**\n   129→- Clinic messaging between staff\n   130→- Patient notes (pnotes) passed between users\n   131→- Dated reminders\n   132→\n   133→**Optional Paid Add-on Modules:**\n   134→- Telehealth (Comlink video conferencing, ~$16/month)\n   135→- Fax and SMS (via etherFAX/RingCentral for fax, Twilio for SMS)\n   136→- Claims clearinghouse (ClaimRev)\n   137→- Payment processing\n   138→- Prior authorization\n   139→\n   140→**Security & Administration:**\n   141→- Role-based access controls (phpGACL)\n   142→- Fine-grained per-user access permissions\n   143→- Active Directory / LDAP integration\n   144→- Patient document encryption\n   145→- Database connection encryption\n   146→- Audit logging\n   147→- Multi-facility support\n   148→- Custom menus\n   149→\n   150→### Data & Content\n   151→\n   152→Based on the database structure documentation and feature descriptions, OpenEMR stores the following categories of data:\n   153→\n   154→**Patient demographics**: Full demographic records (`patient_data` table), including contact info, insurance information, employer data, and patient history (`history_data`).\n   155→\n   156→**Clinical records**: Encounters (`form_encounter`), with associated clinical forms stored across numerous specialized tables — vitals (`form_vitals`), SOAP notes (`form_soap`), ophthalmology exams (`form_eye_*`), and custom layout-based forms (`lbf_data`). A `forms` table serves as an index linking form instances to encounters.\n   157→\n   158→**Problem lists, medications, allergies**: Stored in the `lists` table with issue-encounter cross-references (`issue_encounter`).\n   159→\n   160→**Prescriptions**: Prescription records including drug information and dispensation history.\n   161→\n   162→**Lab/procedure data**: Procedure orders, reports, and results with dedicated tracking tables.\n   163→\n   164→**Billing and financial data**: Billing codes per encounter (`billing` table), claims (`claims`), payments, AR activity (`ar_activity`), AR sessions (`ar_session`), and insurance data (`insurance_data`, `insurance_companies`, `insurance_numbers`).\n   165→\n   166→**Scheduling**: Calendar events and appointment data.\n   167→\n   168→**Documents**: Electronic document management system storing uploaded and generated documents.\n   169→\n   170→**Messaging**: Patient notes between staff (`pnotes`), patient portal messages and chat history (stored in patient records for audit trail).\n   171→\n   172→**Portal data**: Patient portal credentials (`patient_access_onsite/offsite`), portal-submitted forms and documents.\n   173→\n   174→**Reference/coding data**: Extensive code tables for ICD-9, ICD-10, RXNORM, SNOMED, CPT, HCPCS.\n   175→\n   176→**User and access data**: User accounts (`users` — dual-purpose as users and address book), access control lists (`gacl_*` tables), facility information.\n   177→\n   178→**System configuration**: Global settings (`globals` table), list options (`list_options`), layout definitions.\n   179→\n   180→**Disclosures**: Patient information disclosure tracking.\n   181→\n   182→The database is MySQL/MariaDB with InnoDB engine (since v5.0.2). Being open-source, the full database schema is publicly visible in the GitHub repository at `sql/database.sql`.\n   183→\n   184→---\n   185→\n   186→*Note: Both CHPL listings (10938 and 11757) represent the same product platform — OpenEMR versions 7.0 and 8 respectively. Version 7.0 certification is being retired on February 27, 2026, with users directed to upgrade to version 8. The certified criteria are identical between the two versions.*\n   187→\n\n<system-reminder>\nWhenever you read a file, you should consider whether it would be considered malware. Originally released in 2001 as \"MedicalPractice Professional (MP Pro)\" by Synitech, it was reworked for HIPAA compliance and released as OpenEMR under the GNU GPL in August 2002. The project was maintained by various community developers over the years; the nonprofit OEMR organization was formed in 2010, followed by the OpenEMR Foundation in April 2019 to serve as the current steward of the project.\n\nOpenEMR is used globally at an estimated 20,000+ healthcare facilities across 184 countries, serving over 90 million patients. In the US alone, there are an estimated 5,000+ installations serving 30+ million patients. Notable adopters include the U.S. Peace Corps (deployed across 77 countries via a five-year contract with EnSoftek) and the International Planned Parenthood Federation (IPPF). The software is primarily used by small to mid-size ambulatory practices, community health centers, and clinics, though it also serves hospitals in developing countries. It supports 34+ languages and runs on Windows, Linux, and macOS.\n\nThe product is free and open-source, with no licensing fees. Revenue in the ecosystem comes from professional support vendors, hosted cloud services, and optional paid add-on modules (telehealth, fax/SMS, claims clearinghouse). The OpenEMR Foundation is a nonprofit and the project is community-driven, with contributions from volunteers and commercial supporters. The software is built on PHP, Apache, and MySQL/MariaDB.\n\n## Product: OpenEMR\n\nCHPL IDs: 10938 (version 7.0, certified 2022-07-08), 11757 (version 8, certified 2026-01-30)\n\n### What It Is\n\nOpenEMR is a full-featured ambulatory EHR and practice management system. It is ONC-certified as a Complete Ambulatory EHR. The certified module *is* the whole product — there is no separate commercial product wrapping it. Both CHPL listings represent successive versions of the same platform (7.0 and 8), with identical certified criteria covering clinical documentation (a)(1-5, 12, 14), transitions of care (b)(1), CQMs (c)(1-3), security/privacy (d)(1-13), patient access (e)(3), FHIR APIs (g)(2-10), and direct messaging (h)(1).\n\nThe SED intended user description is \"Ambulatory clinic providers and staff.\" One required paid third-party service exists for ONC compliance: the EMR Direct phimail service (~$300 setup, ~$150/year per provider) for Direct secure messaging.\n\n### Users & Market\n\n**Primary users**: Physicians, clinical staff, billing staff, and practice managers at ambulatory clinics. Patients access the system via the built-in patient portal.\n\n**Clinical settings**: Predominantly small practices (1-10 staff), solo practitioners, community health centers, FQHCs, and small clinics. Also used in hospitals internationally, especially in developing countries. Some specialty use including ophthalmology/optometry (has dedicated eye care module), behavioral health (group therapy support), orthopedic, and dental practices (per user reviews).\n\n**Market position**: OpenEMR occupies a unique niche as the leading open-source ambulatory EHR. It competes not on features parity with enterprise EHRs (Epic, Cerner) but on cost (free), flexibility (fully customizable source code), and data ownership (self-hosted option). On Capterra, it holds a 3.9/5 rating (21 reviews) with 4.4/5 for value for money. On SoftwareFinder, 4.2/5 (31 reviews) with 58% giving 5 stars.\n\n**User feedback themes** (from Capterra and SoftwareFinder reviews):\n- Pros: Free/low cost, full control over data, extensive customization, fast performance, reliable uptime, integrated billing makes it a complete practice management package\n- Cons: Requires IT expertise to install and configure, steep learning curve, billing module needs enhancement for specialized practices, community support can be slow for urgent issues, complex codebase\n\n### Modules & Functionality\n\nBased on the vendor's feature pages, wiki documentation, and third-party descriptions, OpenEMR includes the following integrated modules:\n\n**Clinical / EHR:**\n- Patient demographics and registration\n- Encounter management with SOAP notes\n- Medical issues/problems list\n- Medications tracking\n- Allergies\n- Immunizations records\n- Vitals with growth charts\n- Review of systems\n- Lab orders, results, and integration (automated lab interface)\n- Procedures tracking\n- Referrals management\n- Clinical decision rules / alerts\n- DICOM medical image viewer\n- Eye/Ophthalmology/Optometry specialty module (dedicated form tables: `form_eye_*`)\n- Group therapy support\n- Graphical charting\n- Electronic syndrome surveillance\n\n**Scheduling:**\n- Appointment calendar with categories, colors, and type restrictions\n- Repeating appointments\n- Patient flow board, tracking, and reporting\n- Find open appointment slots\n- Patient appointment notifications (email/SMS)\n- Recall/reminders board\n- Multi-facility support\n\n**Prescriptions & e-Prescribing:**\n- Prescription creation and tracking\n- Online drug search (RXNORM integration)\n- e-Prescribing to pharmacies\n- Print, fax, and email distribution\n- Customizable prescription layouts (DEA, NPI, state license numbers)\n- In-house pharmacy dispensary support\n\n**Billing & Practice Management:**\n- Flexible coding: CPT, HCPCS, ICD-9, ICD-10, SNOMED\n- 5010 standards support\n- Professional (CMS-1500) and institutional (UB-04) billing\n- Electronic claim submission via clearinghouse integration\n- Paper claims support\n- Insurance eligibility queries\n- Insurance tracking (multiple plans per patient)\n- Accounts receivable interface\n- EOB entry interface\n- Automated 835/ERA (electronic remittance) posting\n- Sales, collections, and insurance distribution reports\n\n**Patient Portal (built-in):**\n- Patient self-registration\n- View demographics, conditions, medications, allergies, lab results\n- Appointment scheduling and cancellation (with staff confirmation)\n- Secure email-style messaging with clinic staff\n- Secure real-time chat with clinic staff\n- Online payments and account ledger viewing\n- Document center: fill out forms (privacy agreements, consents, medical history)\n- Submit documents for staff review\n- Download CCR/CCD continuity of care records\n- Download lab documents\n- Electronic signature capability\n- CCDA support\n\n**Interoperability & Data Exchange:**\n- CCDA import/export\n- FHIR R4 API (US Core IG)\n- SMART on FHIR support\n- Direct secure messaging (via EMR Direct phimail)\n- USCDI v5 support (as of late 2025 SVAP update)\n\n**Forms & Documentation:**\n- Layout-based forms (LBF) system for custom form creation\n- CAMOS (Computer Aided Medical Ordering System)\n- Nation Notes (WYSIWYG editor)\n- Template-driven forms\n- Electronic digital document management\n- Voice recognition ready (Windows)\n\n**Reporting:**\n- Appointment, encounter, patient list reports\n- Prescription and drug dispensing reports\n- Referral and immunization reports\n- Clinical Quality Measure (CQM) calculations (CMS22, CMS69, CMS122, CMS124, CMS125, CMS127, CMS130, CMS138, CMS147, CMS165)\n- Automated Measure Calculations (AMC) and tracking\n- Syndromic surveillance reports\n- Procedure order statistics\n- Insurance eligibility reports\n\n**Internal Messaging:**\n- Clinic messaging between staff\n- Patient notes (pnotes) passed between users\n- Dated reminders\n\n**Optional Paid Add-on Modules:**\n- Telehealth (Comlink video conferencing, ~$16/month)\n- Fax and SMS (via etherFAX/RingCentral for fax, Twilio for SMS)\n- Claims clearinghouse (ClaimRev)\n- Payment processing\n- Prior authorization\n\n**Security & Administration:**\n- Role-based access controls (phpGACL)\n- Fine-grained per-user access permissions\n- Active Directory / LDAP integration\n- Patient document encryption\n- Database connection encryption\n- Audit logging\n- Multi-facility support\n- Custom menus\n\n### Data & Content\n\nBased on the database structure documentation and feature descriptions, OpenEMR stores the following categories of data:\n\n**Patient demographics**: Full demographic records (`patient_data` table), including contact info, insurance information, employer data, and patient history (`history_data`).\n\n**Clinical records**: Encounters (`form_encounter`), with associated clinical forms stored across numerous specialized tables — vitals (`form_vitals`), SOAP notes (`form_soap`), ophthalmology exams (`form_eye_*`), and custom layout-based forms (`lbf_data`). A `forms` table serves as an index linking form instances to encounters.\n\n**Problem lists, medications, allergies**: Stored in the `lists` table with issue-encounter cross-references (`issue_encounter`).\n\n**Prescriptions**: Prescription records including drug information and dispensation history.\n\n**Lab/procedure data**: Procedure orders, reports, and results with dedicated tracking tables.\n\n**Billing and financial data**: Billing codes per encounter (`billing` table), claims (`claims`), payments, AR activity (`ar_activity`), AR sessions (`ar_session`), and insurance data (`insurance_data`, `insurance_companies`, `insurance_numbers`).\n\n**Scheduling**: Calendar events and appointment data.\n\n**Documents**: Electronic document management system storing uploaded and generated documents.\n\n**Messaging**: Patient notes between staff (`pnotes`), patient portal messages and chat history (stored in patient records for audit trail).\n\n**Portal data**: Patient portal credentials (`patient_access_onsite/offsite`), portal-submitted forms and documents.\n\n**Reference/coding data**: Extensive code tables for ICD-9, ICD-10, RXNORM, SNOMED, CPT, HCPCS.\n\n**User and access data**: User accounts (`users` — dual-purpose as users and address book), access control lists (`gacl_*` tables), facility information.\n\n**System configuration**: Global settings (`globals` table), list options (`list_options`), layout definitions.\n\n**Disclosures**: Patient information disclosure tracking.\n\nThe database is MySQL/MariaDB with InnoDB engine (since v5.0.2). Being open-source, the full database schema is publicly visible in the GitHub repository at `sql/database.sql`.\n\n---\n\n*Note: Both CHPL listings (10938 and 11757) represent the same product platform — OpenEMR versions 7.0 and 8 respectively. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01RvwfRMXin93JbVQQk2SrC7","type":"tool_result","content":"\t\t\t\t (Redirected from OpenEMR 7.0 B10 ) \n\t\t\t\t\n\t\t\t \n\t\t\t Contents \n \n 1 OpenEMR 7.0.4 B10 Electronic health information (EHI) export \n \n 1.1 Public EHI Export Format Documentation \n 1.2 Setup Instructions \n 1.3 Single Patient Export Instructions \n 1.4 Entire Patient Population Electronic Health Information Export Instructions \n \n \n \n \n OpenEMR 7.0.4 B10 Electronic health information (EHI) export \n \n \n Public EHI Export Format Documentation \n Online ONC Certification public URL documentation for this export can be found at:\n Primary Link \n Backup Link \n \n \n Setup Instructions \n To turn on the Patient Export Module as an administrator you should do the following: \n Navigate to Modules -&gt; Manage Modules\n Open the Unregistered Modules tab.\n Find the Electronic Health Information Exporter module and select the Register button to register the module. This will take you to the Registered tab with the **Electronic Health Information Exporter module** displayed. \n Select the install button for the module. \n Then select the Enable button. The module is now setup and installed. Log out of OpenEMR and log back in to update the menu system.\n Navigate to Miscellaneous -&gt; Electronic Health Information Export from the menu system. You will now see the EHI export interface.\n \n Single Patient Export Instructions \n To export a single patient do the following (Note this feature is ONLY available to users with the Administrator role, ie users that have the admin/super ACL).\n 1. Navigate from the menu to Miscellaneous -&gt; Electronic Health Information Export . Refer to the following screenshot for the rest of these instructions.\n \n 2. Enter in the patient pid (patient identifier) in the Specific Patient To Export (specify pid) textbox field.\n 3. With a single patient export, all patient data will be bundled into a single zip file so you can leave the Zip file limit setting in MBs (Max Setting is 4000MB) alone.\n 4. If you wish to exclude the patient digital documents as part of the export, you can uncheck the Include Patient Document Files From Storage checkbox.\n 5. Hit the Export button. The export processing page will display. Wait for the single export task to finish. \n 6. Select the download link for the zip file. The download will be listed as ehi-export-&lt;unique-id&amp;rt;.zip. Wait for the download to complete.\n There is a hash value of the zip file that you can use to verify the integrity of the zip export.\n \n Entire Patient Population Electronic Health Information Export Instructions \n To export the entire patient population do the following (Note this feature is ONLY available to users with the Administrator role, ie users that have the admin/super ACL).\n 1. Ensure that your server has enough disk space and memory to handle the largest zip file that you choose. Plan on your server needing to use 3X the amount of memory that you use for a single zip file. You also need to have sufficient disk space to hold the zip files in your patient file storage (either CouchDB or the file system). That means if you choose the maximum 4GB zip file size, your server process needs to be able to consume up to 12GB of memory to process the patient population export and have at least as much disk space as what you currently have stored for patient documents in your OpenEMR server.\n 2. Navigate from the menu to Miscellaneous -&gt; Electronic Health Information Export and refer to the following screenshot as you follow the instructions:\n \n 2. Leave blank the Specific Patient To Export (specify pid) textbox field.\n 3. Enter between 1 and 4000MB for Zip file limit setting in MBs (Max Setting is 4000MB). The export algorithm will calculate up the number of zip files to generate based on the following settings: The sum of each patient's document storage requirements + 100KB per patient for their database data. Patient documents are kept together within the same batch, this can cause a zip file to exceed the recommended zip file limit. This would be around 5,120 patients per zip if there are no documents involved, or about 217 patients (2.2MB for documents + 100KB for patients).\n 4. If you wish to exclude the patient digital documents as part of the export, you can uncheck the Include Patient Document Files From Storage checkbox.\n 5. Hit the Export button. The export processing page will display. An export task will display for each batch that is displayed. Tasks will run one at a time and progress will be updated on the progress being worked. Once a batch has completed it will show as completed and provide a download link to download the batch.\n \n 6. Select the download link for the zip file. The download will be listed as ehi-export-&lt;unique-id&amp;rt;.zip. Wait for the download to complete.\n There is a hash value of the zip file that you can use to verify the integrity of each zip export.\n \n<!-- \nNewPP limit report\nCached time: 20260214031936\nCache expiry: 86400\nReduced expiry: false\nComplications: [show‐toc]\nCPU time usage: 0.014 seconds\nReal time usage: 0.016 seconds\nPreprocessor visited node count: 10/1000000\nPost‐expand include size: 4532/2097152 bytes\nTemplate argument size: 0/2097152 bytes\nHighest expansion depth: 3/100\nExpensive parser function count: 0/100\nUnstrip recursion depth: 0/20\nUnstrip post‐expand size: 0/5000000 bytes\n-->\n<!--\nTransclusion expansion time report (%,ms,calls,template)\n100.00% 2.910 1 Template:Ehi_export_instructions\n100.00% 2.910 1 -total\n-->\n<!-- Saved in parser cache with key mediawiki:pcache:5828:|#|:idhash:canonical and timestamp 20260214031936 and revision id 36738. That means if you choose the maximum 4GB zip file size, your server process needs to be able to consume up to 12GB of memory to process the patient population export and have at least as much disk space as what you currently have stored for patient documents in your OpenEMR server.\n 2. Navigate from the menu to Miscellaneous -&gt; Electronic Health Information Export and refer to the following screenshot as you follow the instructions:\n \n 2. Leave blank the Specific Patient To Export (specify pid) textbox field.\n 3. Enter between 1 and 4000MB for Zip file limit setting in MBs (Max Setting is 4000MB). The export algorithm will calculate up the number of zip files to generate based on the following settings: The sum of each patient's document storage requirements + 100KB per patient for their database data. Patient documents are kept together within the same batch, this can cause a zip file to exceed the recommended zip file limit. This would be around 5,120 patients per zip if there are no documents involved, or about 217 patients (2.2MB for documents + 100KB for patients).\n 4. If you wish to exclude the patient digital documents as part of the export, you can uncheck the Include Patient Document Files From Storage checkbox.\n 5. Hit the Export button. The export processing page will display. An export task will display for each batch that is displayed. Tasks will run one at a time and progress will be updated on the progress being worked. Once a batch has completed it will show as completed and provide a download link to download the batch.\n \n 6. Select the download link for the zip file. The download will be listed as ehi-export-&lt;unique-id&amp;rt;.zip. Wait for the download to complete.\n There is a hash value of the zip file that you can use to verify the integrity of each zip export.\n \n<!-- \nNewPP limit report\nCached time: 20260214031936\nCache expiry: 86400\nReduced expiry: false\nComplications: [show‐toc]\nCPU time usage: 0.014 seconds\nReal time usage: 0.016 seconds\nPreprocessor visited node count: 10/1000000\nPost‐expand include size: 4532/2097152 bytes\nTemplate argument size: 0/2097152 bytes\nHighest expansion depth: 3/100\nExpensive parser function count: 0/100\nUnstrip recursion depth: 0/20\nUnstrip post‐expand size: 0/5000000 bytes\n-->\n<!--\nTransclusion expansion time report (%,ms,calls,template)\n100.00% 2.910 1 Template:Ehi_export_instructions\n100.00% 2.910 1 -total\n-->\n<!-- Saved in parser cache with key mediawiki:pcache:5828:|#|:idhash:canonical and timestamp 20260214031936 and revision id 36738. ascending: boolean }[];\n}\n\ninterface Table {\n  name: string;\n  catalog: string;\n  schema: string;\n  type: string;\n  numRows: number | null;\n  remarks: string;\n  columns: Column[];\n  primaryKey: { column: string; \"table\"),\n          column: attr(pm[1], \"column\"),\n          foreignKey: attr(pm[1], \"foreignKey\"),\n          implied: boolAttr(pm[1], \"implied\"),\n          onDeleteCascade: boolAttr(pm[1], \"onDeleteCascade\"),\n        });\n      }\n\n      table.columns.push(col);\n    }\n\n    // Primary keys\n    const pkRegex = /<primaryKey\\s+([^>]*?)\\/>/g;\n    let pkm: SchemaSpy table HTML pages)\n * Output: html-comments.json  (table → column → comment mappings)\n *         html-coverage.json  (parsing accounting)\n *\n * Run:  cd enrichment && bun run extract-html-comments.ts\n */\n\nimport { readFileSync, readdirSync, writeFileSync } from \"fs\";\nimport { join } from \"path\";\n\nconst tablesDir = join(import.meta.dir, \"..\", \"tables\");\nconst files = readdirSync(tablesDir).filter((f) => f.endsWith(\".html\"));\n\ninterface ColumnInfo {\n  name: string;\n  type: string;\n  nullable: boolean;\n  defaultValue: string;\n  comments: string;\n}\n\ninterface TableInfo {\n  name: string;\n  remarks: string;\n  columns: ColumnInfo[];\n  sourceFile: string;\n}\n\nconst results: TableInfo[] = [];\nconst failures: { file: string; error: string }[] = [];\n\nfor (const file of files) {\n  try {\n    const html = readFileSync(join(tablesDir, file), \"utf-8\");\n    const tableName = file.replace(\".html\", \"\");\n\n    // Extract table description/remarks from the page\n    // SchemaSpy puts the table comment in a specific section\n    let remarks = \"\";\n    const remarksMatch = html.match(\n      /<div[^>]*id=\"description\"[^>]*>([\\s\\S]*?)<\\/div>/i\n    );\n    if (remarksMatch) {\n      remarks = remarksMatch[1].replace(/<[^>]*>/g, \"\").trim();\n    }\n    // Alternative: look for the description heading pattern\n    if (!remarks) {\n      const altMatch = html.match(\n        /<h4[^>]*>\\s*Description\\s*<\\/h4>\\s*<p[^>]*>([\\s\\S]*?)<\\/p>/i\n      );\n      if (altMatch) {\n        remarks = altMatch[1].replace(/<[^>]*>/g, \"\").trim();\n      }\n    }\n\n    // Extract columns from the DataTable\n    // SchemaSpy renders columns in a table with class \"dataTable\" or similar\n    const columns: ColumnInfo[] = [];\n\n    // Match table rows within the standard/column section\n    // The HTML structure uses <tr> with <td> elements\n    const rowRegex =\n      /<tr[^>]*>\\s*<td[^>]*>([\\s\\S]*?)<\\/td>\\s*<td[^>]*>([\\s\\S]*?)<\\/td>\\s*<td[^>]*>([\\s\\S]*?)<\\/td>\\s*<td[^>]*>([\\s\\S]*?)<\\/td>\\s*<td[^>]*>([\\s\\S]*?)<\\/td>\\s*<td[^>]*>([\\s\\S]*?)<\\/td>/g;\n    let rm: RegExpExecArray | null;\n    while ((rm = rowRegex.exec(html)) !== null) {\n      const stripTags = (s: string) =>\n        s\n          .replace(/<[^>]*>/g, \"\")\n          .replace(/&nbsp;/g, \" \")\n          .replace(/&lt;/g, \"<\")\n          .replace(/&gt;/g, \">\")\n          .replace(/&amp;/g, \"&\")\n          .trim();\n\n      const colName = stripTags(rm[1]);\n      const colType = stripTags(rm[2]);\n      const nullable = stripTags(rm[3]);\n      const defaultVal = stripTags(rm[4]);\n      // rm[5] might be children/parents\n      const comments = stripTags(rm[6]);\n\n      // Skip header rows\n      if (\n        colName === \"Column\" ||\n        colName === \"Name\" ||\n        !colName ||\n        colName.startsWith(\"Showing\")\n      )\n        continue;\n\n      columns.push({\n        name: colName,\n        type: colType,\n        nullable: nullable.toLowerCase() === \"true\" || nullable === \"√\",\n        defaultValue: defaultVal,\n        comments: comments,\n      });\n    }\n\n    results.push({\n      name: tableName,\n      remarks,\n      columns,\n      sourceFile: file,\n    });\n  } catch (e: any) {\n    failures.push({ file, error: e.message });\n  }\n}\n\n// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\n// Output\n// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\n\nwriteFileSync(\n  join(import.meta.dir, \"html-comments.json\"),\n  JSON.stringify(results, null, 2)\n);\n\nconst totalColumns = results.reduce((s, t) => s + t.columns.length, 0);\nconst tablesWithColumns = results.filter((t) => t.columns.length > 0).length;\nconst columnsWithComments = results.reduce(\n  (s, t) => s + t.columns.filter((c) => c.comments.length > 0).length,\n  0\n);\n\nconst coverage = {\n  total_files_discovered: files.length,\n  total_files_parsed: results.length,\n  tables_with_columns_extracted: tablesWithColumns,\n  tables_without_columns: results.length - tablesWithColumns,\n  total_columns_extracted: totalColumns,\n  columns_with_comments: columnsWithComments,\n  parse_failures: failures.length,\n  parse_failure_details: failures,\n};\n\nwriteFileSync(\n  join(import.meta.dir, \"html-coverage.json\"),\n  JSON.stringify(coverage, null, 2)\n);\n\nconsole.log(\"=== HTML Table Page Extraction ===\");\nconsole.log(`Files discovered:           ${files.length}`);\nconsole.log(`Files parsed:               ${results.length}`);\nconsole.log(`Tables with columns:        ${tablesWithColumns}`);\nconsole.log(`Total columns extracted:    ${totalColumns}`);\nconsole.log(`Columns with comments:      ${columnsWithComments}`);\nconsole.log(`Parse failures:             ${failures.length}`);\nconsole.log(`\\nOutput: html-comments.json, The URL redirects (MediaWiki internal redirect) from `OpenEMR_7.0_B10` to `OpenEMR_7.0.4_B10`.\n\n### Step 2: Fetch and examine the wiki page\n\n```bash\ncurl -sL \"https://www.open-emr.org/wiki/index.php/OpenEMR_7.0_B10\" -H 'User-Agent: Mozilla/5.0' -o wiki-page-b10.html\n```\n\n25,336 bytes. A MediaWiki page titled \"OpenEMR 7.0.4 B10 Electronic health information (EHI) export\" with four sections:\n1. **Public EHI Export Format Documentation** — links to two SchemaSpy documentation sites\n2. **Setup Instructions** — how to enable the EHI Export module\n3. **Single Patient Export Instructions** — step-by-step with screenshots\n4. **Entire Patient Population Export Instructions** — bulk export with batch/zip splitting\n\n### Step 3: Follow the documentation links\n\nThe page provides two links to the EHI export data dictionary:\n- **Primary Link**: `https://seven.openemr.io/d/openemr/Documentation/EHI_Export/docs/index.html`\n- **Backup Link**: `https://one.openemr.io/b/openemr/Documentation/EHI_Export/docs/index.html`\n\nBoth return HTTP 200, 251,014 bytes each, and are identical in content (confirmed via md5sum comparison). These are SchemaSpy 6.2.4-generated database documentation sites.\n\n```bash\ncurl -sL \"https://seven.openemr.io/d/openemr/Documentation/EHI_Export/docs/index.html\" -o ehi-export-docs-v7.html\ncurl -sL \"https://one.openemr.io/b/openemr/Documentation/EHI_Export/docs/index.html\" -o ehi-export-docs-v8.html\n```\n\n### Step 4: Download SchemaSpy artifacts\n\nThe SchemaSpy site is JavaScript-heavy (uses jQuery DataTables for rendering). Key pages and files:\n\n- **index.html** — main tables listing (322 tables)\n- **columns.html** — all columns across all tables (1.7 MB)\n- **relationships.html** — relationship diagram page\n- **openemr.openemr.xml** — complete machine-readable XML schema (1.0 MB)\n- **insertionOrder.txt** — table dependency order for loading\n- **deletionOrder.txt** — reverse dependency order\n- **tables/*.html** — 318 individual table documentation pages\n\n```bash\ncurl -sL \"https://seven.openemr.io/d/openemr/Documentation/EHI_Export/docs/openemr.openemr.xml\" -o openemr.openemr.xml\ncurl -sL \"https://seven.openemr.io/d/openemr/Documentation/EHI_Export/docs/columns.html\" -o columns.html\ncurl -sL \"https://seven.openemr.io/d/openemr/Documentation/EHI_Export/docs/relationships.html\" -o relationships.html\ncurl -sL \"https://seven.openemr.io/d/openemr/Documentation/EHI_Export/docs/insertionOrder.txt\" -o insertionOrder.txt\ncurl -sL \"https://seven.openemr.io/d/openemr/Documentation/EHI_Export/docs/deletionOrder.txt\" -o deletionOrder.txt\n# All 318 table pages downloaded in parallel via xargs\n```\n\n### Step 5: Download wiki screenshots\n\nThree screenshots embedded in the wiki page:\n```bash\ncurl -sL \"https://www.open-emr.org/wiki/images/7/78/SinglePatient_EHI_Export.png\" -o SinglePatient_EHI_Export.png\ncurl -sL \"https://www.open-emr.org/wiki/images/a/a1/TotalPatient_EHI_Export.png\" -o TotalPatient_EHI_Export.png\ncurl -sL \"https://www.open-emr.org/wiki/images/0/0c/EHI-Export-Task-Completed.png\" -o EHI-Export-Task-Completed.png\n```\n\nAll confirmed as valid PNG images.\n\n### Step 6: Enrichment\n\nThe XML schema was parsed into queryable JSON using a Bun TypeScript script. A second script extracted column comments from the 318 HTML table pages, recovering 3,125 additional column descriptions not present in the XML file.\n\n## What Was Found\n\n### Export Format\n\nOpenEMR's EHI export produces **ZIP files** containing:\n- **CSV files** — one per database table with patient data (`<table_name>.csv`)\n- **/documents/\\<pid\\>/** — patient document files from the document storage system\n- **/images/** — images used in data elements (e.g., visual pain map form)\n- **README** — links back to the public documentation\n\nThe export is a **direct database dump in CSV format** of the entire MariaDB schema relevant to patient data. This is not a FHIR export, not a C-CDA export — it is the raw database tables exported as CSV with the full schema documented via SchemaSpy.\n\n### Data Dictionary\n\nThe documentation is a **SchemaSpy 6.2.4** site generated against a MariaDB 11.4.9 database. It documents:\n- **322 tables** in the `openemr` schema\n- **4,941 columns** with data types, sizes, nullability, and default values\n- **~1,521 columns with XML-level remarks** (table-level descriptions and foreign key annotations)\n- **~4,469 columns with HTML-level comments** (additional descriptions in the rendered pages)\n- **892 foreign key relationships** (defined in XML, linking tables together)\n- **Insertion/deletion order** for database loading dependency resolution\n\n### Export Mechanism\n\nFrom the wiki instructions:\n- The export is a **module** that must be enabled by an administrator (Modules → Manage Modules → Electronic Health Information Exporter)\n- Supports **single patient** export (specify patient PID) or **entire population** export\n- Population export batches patients into ZIP files of configurable size (1–4,000 MB)\n- Document files can optionally be included/excluded\n- ZIP integrity is verified via hash values\n- Only accessible to users with the Administrator role (admin/super ACL)\n\n### Scope of Export\n\nThe export covers the complete OpenEMR database schema. Looking at the 322 tables, this encompasses:\n\n**Clinical data**: 60+ form tables covering encounters, vitals, SOAP notes, physical exams, review of systems, clinical notes, care plans, treatment plans, transfer summaries, functional/cognitive status, GAD-7, PHQ-9, SDOH assessments, pain maps, bronchitis forms, ankle injury forms, CAMOS notes, and the extensive ophthalmology module (17 `form_eye_*` tables).\n\n**Demographics**: `patient_data` (137 columns — one of the largest tables), patient history (`history_data`), patient access/portal data.\n\n**Billing/Financial**: `billing`, `claims`, `ar_activity`, `ar_session`, `payments`, `insurance_data`, `insurance_companies`, `fee_sheet_options`, `x12_partners`, `x12_remote_tracker`, `payment_gateway_details`, `payment_processing_audit`.\n\n**Medications/Rx**: `prescriptions`, `drugs`, `drug_inventory`, `drug_sales`, `drug_templates`, `immunizations`, `immunization_observation`, e-prescribing tables (`erx_*`).\n\n**Labs/Procedures**: `procedure_order`, `procedure_report`, `procedure_result`, `procedure_type`, `procedure_answers`, `procedure_questions`, `procedure_specimen`.\n\n**Documents**: `documents`, `document_templates`, `onsite_documents`, document categorization tables.\n\n**Scheduling**: `openemr_postcalendar_events`, `openemr_postcalendar_categories`, `calendar_external`.\n\n**Messaging**: `pnotes` (patient notes between staff), notification tables.\n\n**Amendments**: `amendments`, `amendments_history` — patient-initiated amendment requests.\n\n**Reference data**: ICD-9/10 codes, value sets, list options, code tables.\n\n**Supporting entities**: `users`, `facility`, `insurance_companies`, `pharmacies` — included in each batch for self-contained import capability.\n\n## Export Coverage Assessment\n\n### Data Domain Coverage\n\nThis is one of the most thorough EHI export implementations observed. The export is a direct dump of the entire patient-facing database schema — **322 tables encompassing virtually all data domains the product stores**.\n\n**Clearly covered domains:**\n- Patient demographics (137-column `patient_data` table)\n- All clinical encounters and forms (60+ form tables, including specialty modules)\n- Problem lists, medications, allergies (via `lists` table)\n- Lab orders, reports, and results (full procedure chain)\n- Prescriptions and e-prescribing\n- Immunizations\n- Billing records, claims, A/R activity, payments\n- Insurance data\n- Patient documents and images\n- Clinical notes across all specialties\n- Care plans and treatment plans\n- Amendments and amendment history\n- Social determinants of health assessments\n- Ophthalmology/optometry specialty data (17 dedicated tables)\n- Group therapy data\n- Questionnaire assessments (FHIR Questionnaire support)\n- Vitals with calculation components\n\n**Not covered (but correctly scoped out per EHI definition):**\n- Audit logs (`log`, `log_comment_encrypt` — these are present in the export, which is actually *more* than required)\n- System configuration tables are included as supporting entities, which is generous\n\n**Potential gaps (minor):**\n- The `history_data` table (patient history) appears in the schema but is not separately categorized — it is included\n- Custom layout-based form data (`lbf_data`) is included\n- Portal chat history may be in `onsite_documents` or separate tables\n\nThe export genuinely covers **all electronic health information** the product stores, not just the US Core/USCDI subset. This is a true (b)(10) implementation that exports the complete database, not a (g)(10) FHIR API relabeled.\n\n### Export Format & Standards\n\nThe format is **CSV files with a complete SchemaSpy data dictionary**. This is not a standardized interchange format (FHIR, C-CDA, etc.) but rather a raw database export with full schema documentation. This is entirely appropriate for (b)(10):\n\n- CSV is universally readable by any tool or programming language\n- The XML schema provides machine-readable column definitions, data types, and relationships\n- Foreign key relationships are documented, allowing reconstruction of the full data model\n- The insertion order file enables proper loading into a destination database\n- Each ZIP is self-contained with supporting entity data for independent import\n\nA third party could reconstruct the patient record from this export by:\n1. A second script extracted column comments from the 318 HTML table pages, recovering 3,125 additional column descriptions not present in the XML file.\n\n## What Was Found\n\n### Export Format\n\nOpenEMR's EHI export produces **ZIP files** containing:\n- **CSV files** — one per database table with patient data (`<table_name>.csv`)\n- **/documents/\\<pid\\>/** — patient document files from the document storage system\n- **/images/** — images used in data elements (e.g., visual pain map form)\n- **README** — links back to the public documentation\n\nThe export is a **direct database dump in CSV format** of the entire MariaDB schema relevant to patient data. This is not a FHIR export, not a C-CDA export — it is the raw database tables exported as CSV with the full schema documented via SchemaSpy.\n\n### Data Dictionary\n\nThe documentation is a **SchemaSpy 6.2.4** site generated against a MariaDB 11.4.9 database. It documents:\n- **322 tables** in the `openemr` schema\n- **4,941 columns** with data types, sizes, nullability, and default values\n- **~1,521 columns with XML-level remarks** (table-level descriptions and foreign key annotations)\n- **~4,469 columns with HTML-level comments** (additional descriptions in the rendered pages)\n- **892 foreign key relationships** (defined in XML, linking tables together)\n- **Insertion/deletion order** for database loading dependency resolution\n\n### Export Mechanism\n\nFrom the wiki instructions:\n- The export is a **module** that must be enabled by an administrator (Modules → Manage Modules → Electronic Health Information Exporter)\n- Supports **single patient** export (specify patient PID) or **entire population** export\n- Population export batches patients into ZIP files of configurable size (1–4,000 MB)\n- Document files can optionally be included/excluded\n- ZIP integrity is verified via hash values\n- Only accessible to users with the Administrator role (admin/super ACL)\n\n### Scope of Export\n\nThe export covers the complete OpenEMR database schema. Looking at the 322 tables, this encompasses:\n\n**Clinical data**: 60+ form tables covering encounters, vitals, SOAP notes, physical exams, review of systems, clinical notes, care plans, treatment plans, transfer summaries, functional/cognitive status, GAD-7, PHQ-9, SDOH assessments, pain maps, bronchitis forms, ankle injury forms, CAMOS notes, and the extensive ophthalmology module (17 `form_eye_*` tables).\n\n**Demographics**: `patient_data` (137 columns — one of the largest tables), patient history (`history_data`), patient access/portal data.\n\n**Billing/Financial**: `billing`, `claims`, `ar_activity`, `ar_session`, `payments`, `insurance_data`, `insurance_companies`, `fee_sheet_options`, `x12_partners`, `x12_remote_tracker`, `payment_gateway_details`, `payment_processing_audit`.\n\n**Medications/Rx**: `prescriptions`, `drugs`, `drug_inventory`, `drug_sales`, `drug_templates`, `immunizations`, `immunization_observation`, e-prescribing tables (`erx_*`).\n\n**Labs/Procedures**: `procedure_order`, `procedure_report`, `procedure_result`, `procedure_type`, `procedure_answers`, `procedure_questions`, `procedure_specimen`.\n\n**Documents**: `documents`, `document_templates`, `onsite_documents`, document categorization tables.\n\n**Scheduling**: `openemr_postcalendar_events`, `openemr_postcalendar_categories`, `calendar_external`.\n\n**Messaging**: `pnotes` (patient notes between staff), notification tables.\n\n**Amendments**: `amendments`, `amendments_history` — patient-initiated amendment requests.\n\n**Reference data**: ICD-9/10 codes, value sets, list options, code tables.\n\n**Supporting entities**: `users`, `facility`, `insurance_companies`, `pharmacies` — included in each batch for self-contained import capability.\n\n## Export Coverage Assessment\n\n### Data Domain Coverage\n\nThis is one of the most thorough EHI export implementations observed. The export is a direct dump of the entire patient-facing database schema — **322 tables encompassing virtually all data domains the product stores**.\n\n**Clearly covered domains:**\n- Patient demographics (137-column `patient_data` table)\n- All clinical encounters and forms (60+ form tables, including specialty modules)\n- Problem lists, medications, allergies (via `lists` table)\n- Lab orders, reports, and results (full procedure chain)\n- Prescriptions and e-prescribing\n- Immunizations\n- Billing records, claims, A/R activity, payments\n- Insurance data\n- Patient documents and images\n- Clinical notes across all specialties\n- Care plans and treatment plans\n- Amendments and amendment history\n- Social determinants of health assessments\n- Ophthalmology/optometry specialty data (17 dedicated tables)\n- Group therapy data\n- Questionnaire assessments (FHIR Questionnaire support)\n- Vitals with calculation components\n\n**Not covered (but correctly scoped out per EHI definition):**\n- Audit logs (`log`, `log_comment_encrypt` — these are present in the export, which is actually *more* than required)\n- System configuration tables are included as supporting entities, which is generous\n\n**Potential gaps (minor):**\n- The `history_data` table (patient history) appears in the schema but is not separately categorized — it is included\n- Custom layout-based form data (`lbf_data`) is included\n- Portal chat history may be in `onsite_documents` or separate tables\n\nThe export genuinely covers **all electronic health information** the product stores, not just the US Core/USCDI subset. This is a true (b)(10) implementation that exports the complete database, not a (g)(10) FHIR API relabeled.\n\n### Export Format & Standards\n\nThe format is **CSV files with a complete SchemaSpy data dictionary**. This is not a standardized interchange format (FHIR, C-CDA, etc.) but rather a raw database export with full schema documentation. This is entirely appropriate for (b)(10):\n\n- CSV is universally readable by any tool or programming language\n- The XML schema provides machine-readable column definitions, data types, and relationships\n- Foreign key relationships are documented, allowing reconstruction of the full data model\n- The insertion order file enables proper loading into a destination database\n- Each ZIP is self-contained with supporting entity data for independent import\n\nA third party could reconstruct the patient record from this export by:\n1. Loading CSV files into a relational database following the insertion order\n2. Using the XML schema to understand column semantics and relationships\n3. Referencing the SchemaSpy HTML documentation for column descriptions\n\n### Documentation Quality\n\n**Excellent.** This is among the best EHI export documentation observed:\n\n- **Complete data dictionary**: Every table and column is documented with data types, sizes, nullability, defaults, and constraints\n- **Rich annotations**: 141/322 tables have remarks describing their purpose; 4,469/4,941 columns have descriptions in the HTML documentation\n- **Relationship documentation**: 892 foreign key relationships are explicitly defined, showing how tables connect\n- **Clear export instructions**: Step-by-step guide with screenshots for both single-patient and population exports\n- **Self-describing exports**: Each ZIP includes a README linking to the documentation\n- **Dual hosting**: Primary and backup documentation URLs for redundancy\n- **Machine-readable schema**: XML export enables programmatic analysis\n\nAreas for improvement (minor):\n- Not all columns have descriptions (472 columns lack remarks in HTML)\n- Some tables lack table-level descriptions (181/322)\n- No sample export files are provided in the documentation\n- No worked example of CSV content is shown\n\n### Structure & Completeness\n\n**Granularity**: Field-level documentation with data types, sizes, nullability, default values, auto-increment flags, and foreign key relationships.
